Language Models over Canonical Byte-Pair Encodings
Tim Vieira, Tianyu Liu, Clemente Pasti, Yahya Emara, Brian DuSell, Benjamin LeBrun, Mario Giulianelli, Juan Luis Gastaldi, Timothy J. O’Donnell, Ryan Cotterell

Abstract
Modern language models represent probability distributions over character strings as distributions over (shorter) token strings derived via a deterministic tokenizer, such as byte-pair encoding. While this approach is highly effective at scaling up language models to large corpora, its current incarnations have a concerning property: the model assigns nonzero probability mass to an exponential number of noncanonical token encodings of each character string–—these are token strings that decode to valid character strings but are impossible under the deterministic tokenizer (i.e., they will never be seen in any training corpus, no matter how large). This misallocation is both erroneous, as noncanonical strings never appear in training data, and wasteful, diverting probability mass away from plausible outputs. These are avoidable mistakes! In this work, we propose methods to enforce canonicality in token-level language models, ensuring that only canonical token strings are assigned positive probability. We present two approaches: (1) canonicality by conditioning, leveraging test-time inference strategies without additional training, and (2) canonicality by construction, a model parameterization that guarantees canonical outputs but requires training. We demonstrate that fixing canonicality mistakes improves the likelihood of held-out data for several models and corpora. github.com/genlm/canonical-icml-2025



1
Introduction

Figure 1:
The figure shows the canonical and noncanonical encodings of the string
Hello,␣world
. The diagram shows the top-8 token encodings of the string according to their probability (descending top to bottom), as there are hundreds of them for this short string. Note that the canonical token encoding is the most likely one (i.e., it is at the top), which is reassuring as it is the most representative of the training data.

Modern language models are probability distributions over character strings (denoted
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}^{*}}
) that are parameterized as distributions over a different space of
token
strings (denoted
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}
). In the case of the prevalent byte-pair encoding
(
Gage 1994
, BPE;)
, each token may be regarded as a multi-character (or multi-byte) chunk of a UTF-8-encoded string.
Such a language model is trained by
encoding
its training data into token strings, which has the benefit of compressing the large corpus.
The BPE encoding is a pair of functions: an encoding function
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}^{*}}\to{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}
that maps a given input character string
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
to its
canonical encoding

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
, and a decoding function
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}\to{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}^{*}}
that maps any token string
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}
back to a character string
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})
.
The result of training on a corpus of token strings is a language model
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}
that generates a token string
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}
and decodes it to a character string
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
.

We define the set of
canonical
token strings
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}
as those that appear as the canonical encoding of at least one character string, i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}})\mid{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}^{*}}\}
. These are the only strings that are
possible
in the tokenized training data of
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}
. The set of
noncanonical
token strings is, thus,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}\smallsetminus{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
. Even though
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}
has never—and will never—see a noncanonical token string in its training data, it will still place nonzero probability mass on them due to model architecture and training limitations.
Figure

1
shows an example of a string with its canonical encoding and its exponentially many noncanonical encodings.

Fixing canonicality mistakes.

Removing noncanonical strings from the support of the estimated distribution
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}
can improve the likelihood of the correct token strings. We describe two families of methods for doing so

•

Canonicality by conditioning: We explore efficient test-time inference methods for conditionally generating text that satisfies the canonicality constraint without retraining.

•

Canonicality by construction: We explore methods that impose canonicality constraints directly in the language model’s parameterized architecture and give a method to fine-tune its parameters.

In addition to these novel methods, this paper presents the following contributions:

•

We prove that our methods can only improve the fit to the true distribution over tokens.

•

We show empirically that fixing canonicality mistakes using our methods improves the likelihood of held-out data for several models and corpora.

•

As a side effect of pursuing the goal of generating only canonical token strings, we discovered an efficient, incremental canonicality test for BPE (see
Appendix

B
) that is significantly simpler than prior work
(
Berglund et al. 2024
;
Cognetta & Okazaki 2024
, e.g.,)
, as it does not require automata theory to understand or implement.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Strings

An
alphabet
is a non-empty, finite set of
symbols
(e.g., bytes, characters, or tokens) from which we can build strings. A
string
is a finite-length sequence of symbols from a given alphabet. Let
{\Gamma}
be an alphabet. Let
{\Gamma}^{*}
denote the
set of all strings
over
{\Gamma}
.
We use
\left|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right|
to denote the length of the string and
\varepsilon
to denote the string of zero length.
Let
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\prime\prime}
denote the concatenation of
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\prime}
and
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\prime\prime}
.
We write
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{<n}\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}{\gamma}_{1}{\cdots}{\gamma}_{n-1}
to access
the first
n-1
symbols of the string
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
.
Let
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\preceq{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\prime}
denote that
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
is a prefix of
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\prime}
, and
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\prime}\succeq{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
denote that
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\prime}
is has
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
as a prefix.
The
set of bigrams
of a given string
\textsc{bigrams}({\gamma}_{1}{\cdots}{\gamma}_{N})\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}\{{\gamma}_{n}{\gamma}_{n+1}\mid 1\leq n<N\}
.

2.2
Information-Theoretic Quantities

For completeness, we include definitions of the following common information-theoretic quantities, which are measured in bits. Let
\log
be the logarithm function in base
2
throughout this paper. Let
p
and
q
be probability distributions over a countable domain
\mathcal{X}
.

•

Entropy
:

\mathrm{H}(p)\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}-\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)\log p(x)

•

Cross-entropy
:

\mathrm{H}(p,q)\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}-\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)\log q(x)

•

KL divergence
:

\mathrm{KL}(p\|q)=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)\log\frac{p(x)}{q(x)}

These quantities are related via
\mathrm{KL}(p\|q)=\mathrm{H}(p,q)-\mathrm{H}(p)
, and
\mathrm{H}(p)=\mathrm{H}(p,p)
. Lastly, we follow the information-theoretic convention that
0\log 0\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}0
in these definitions.

2.3
Language Models

A language model
p_{{\Gamma}}
is a probability distribution over a set of strings
{\Gamma^{*}}
. Thus, we can define a string-valued random variable
Y\sim p_{{\Gamma}}
with this distribution. Moreover, we can compute the conditional probabilities of different events pertaining to the random variable
Y
, as we normally do in probability theory. However, since
Y
is string-valued, the comparisons and transformations applied to it must be string-friendly. We define the
prefix probability
:

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{p_{{\Gamma}}}({\boldsymbol{\gamma}})\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}\!\!\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}Y\sim p_{{\Gamma}}\end{subarray}}\left[Y\succeq{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right]=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\prime}\in{\Gamma^{*}}}\mathds{1}\{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\prime}\succeq{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\}\,p_{{\Gamma}}({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\prime})

(1a)

and the
conditional prefix probability
:

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{p_{{\Gamma}}}({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\prime}\mid{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}\!\!\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}Y\sim p_{{\Gamma}}\end{subarray}}\left[Y\succeq{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\prime}\,\middle|\,Y\succeq{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right]=\frac{\overrightarrow{p_{{\Gamma}}}({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\prime})}{\overrightarrow{p_{{\Gamma}}}({\boldsymbol{\gamma}})}

(1b)

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{p_{{\Gamma}}}({\textsc{eos}}\mid{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}\frac{p_{{\Gamma}}({\boldsymbol{\gamma}})}{\overrightarrow{p_{{\Gamma}}}({\boldsymbol{\gamma}})}

(1c)

We can use conditional prefix probability to factorize the probability
p_{{\Gamma}}({\boldsymbol{\gamma}})
of a string
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}={\gamma}_{1}{\cdots}{\gamma}_{T}
as the conditional probability of each of its symbols
{\gamma}_{1},{\ldots},{\gamma}_{T}
, followed by an
end-of-string event

eos
:
1
1

1

Here,
eos
is an event,
not
a symbol; thus, it may never appear in any well-formed string.

\displaystyle p_{{\Gamma}}({\boldsymbol{\gamma}})=\overrightarrow{p_{{\Gamma}}}({\textsc{eos}}\mid{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})\prod_{t=1}^{|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}|}\overrightarrow{p_{{\Gamma}}}\left({\gamma}_{t}\mid{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{<t}\right)

(1d)

Note that each conditional prefix distribution
\overrightarrow{p_{{\Gamma}}}\left(\cdot\mid{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{<t}\right)
is a probability distribution over
{\Gamma}\cup\{{\textsc{eos}}\}
. Many language models are
defined
via the product in
Equation

1d
where each single-symbol conditional prefix probability comes from a parametric model trained by predicting the next token given the previous context. Examples of this include transformers
(
Vaswani et al. 2017
)
, RNNs
(
Mikolov et al. 2010
;
Sundermeyer et al. 2015
, e.g.,)
, and
n
-gram models
(
Shannon 1948
, e.g.,)
.

2.4
Tokenized Language Models

An
(exact, deterministic) tokenization model

(
Gastaldi et al. 2025
)
is a tuple
({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa})
where
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}
is an alphabet of characters (or bytes),
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}
is an alphabet of tokens,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}^{*}}\to{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}
is an
encoding function
,
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}\to{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}^{*}}
is a
decoding function
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}))={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
for all
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}^{*}}
.
2
2

2

Note that for
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}
to form a
bijection
between
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}^{*}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}
, we would additionally require
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}))={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}
for all
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}
.

A
tokenized language model
is a language model
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}}
over character strings
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}^{*}}
that is
parameterized
by a token-level language model
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}
over
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}
and a decoding function
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}\to{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}^{*}}
. The tokenized language model defines the following probability distribution over
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}^{*}}
:

\displaystyle p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}})\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}}\mathds{1}\{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})\}\,p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})

(2)

Notice that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}
is conspicuously absent from this expression, as it is only part of the estimation process, which we characterize below. In
Section

4
, we consider alternatives to
Equation

2
that exploit prior knowledge of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}
to force the probability of noncanonical token strings to be zero.
3
3

3

Vieira et al. 2024
give algorithms for working with tokenized language models at the character (or byte) level, including algorithms for computing conditional probabilities.

Estimating a tokenized language model.

Let
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}}^{\star}
be some probability distribution over
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}^{*}}
we wish to estimate. As is standard in statistical estimation, we take a large sample
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(M)}\mathrel{\overset{\text{{{\scriptsize i.i.d.}}}}{\sim}}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}}^{\star}
.
Next, we
encode
each strings of characters into strings of
tokens
, giving us a corpus of token strings:
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}}}^{(m)}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(m)})
for
1\leq m\leq M
. Let
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}^{\star}
denote the distribution of these token strings:

\displaystyle p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}^{\star}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}^{*}}}\mathds{1}\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}}^{\star}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}})

(3)

Next, we estimate a token-level language model
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}
to fit the strings
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}}}^{(M)}
.
After estimation, we have a distribution
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}
over strings of
tokens
rather than strings of
characters
.
Thus, to generate a string of characters, we first generate a token string
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\sim p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}
, and
decode
it (via
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})
) to get a character string. This process is why the tokenized language model’s character strings are distributed according to
Equation

2
.
4
4

4

Gastaldi et al. 2025
characterizes the conditions required for tokenized language models to estimate
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}}^{\star}
consistently.

Tokenization is used in practice because working with (short) token strings is easier than (long) character strings. The most commonly used tokenization scheme for modern language models is byte-pair encoding
(
Sennrich et al. 2016
;
Gage 1994
, BPE; )
, which is a simple and effective algorithm for shortening text
(
Zouhar et al. 2023
)
.

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})

\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{t}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{83}}}}}$}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\,\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{he}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{258}}}}}$}}

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{the}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{1169}}}}}

\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{t}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{83}}}}}$}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\,\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{.}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{13}}}}}$}}

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{the}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{1169}}}}}

\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{tha}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{12898}}}}}$}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\,\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{n}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{77}}}}}$}}

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{than}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{14813}}}}}

\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{tha}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{12898}}}}}$}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\,\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{n}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{77}}}}}$}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\,\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{ks}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{591}}}}}$}}

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{thanks}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{27547}}}}}

\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{Th}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{817}}}}}$}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\,\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{ing}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{278}}}}}$}}

\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{T}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{51}}}}}$}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\,\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{hing}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{722}}}}}$}}

\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{Th}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{817}}}}}$}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\,\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{inging}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{14146}}}}}$}}

\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{T}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{51}}}}}$}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\,\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{hing}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{722}}}}}$}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\,\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{ing}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{278}}}}}$}}

Figure 2:
Short examples of canonical and noncanonical token strings from
GPT2
’s tokenizer. Because the canonicalization operation
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}
is idempotent, each example in the second column is canonical. These examples were chosen to highlight some of BPE’s unintuitive preferences, e.g., BPE is not a longest-match tokenizer, and canonicalization can
increase
the string’s length.

2.5
Byte-Pair Encoding

Byte-pair encoding

(
Gage 1994
;
Sennrich et al. 2016
,
BPE
;)
is a commonly used tokenization scheme.
5
5

5

BPE is the most prevalent tokenization strategy in modern language models; it is used by
OpenAI’s GPT-2
(
Radford et al. 2019
)
, GPT-3
(
Brown et al. 2020
)
, GPT-3.5
(
OpenAI 2022
)
, and GPT-4
(
OpenAI 2024a
)
, GPT-4o
(
OpenAI 2024b
)
;
Meta’s Llama 1
(
Touvron et al. 2023a
)
, Llama 2
(
Touvron et al. 2023b
)
, and Llama 3
(
Llama Team 2024
)
;
Mistral’s 7B
(
Mistral AI 2023
)
, Au
(
Mistral AI 2024a
)
, and Nemo
(
Mistral AI 2024b
)
;
GPT-NeoX
(
Black et al. 2022
)
;
EleutherAI’s Pythia
(
Biderman et al. 2023
)
;
Anthropic’s Claude
(
Anthropic 2023a
)
and Claude 2
(
Anthropic 2023b
)
;
Google’s Gemma
(
Gemma Team 2024
)
; and
AI2’s OLMo 1
(
Groeneveld et al. 2024
)
and OLMo 2
(
Team OLMo 2025
)
.
Intuitively, the encoding function of BPE works as follows: each character of an input string
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\sigma}_{1}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\sigma}_{N}
is initially mapped to an individual token, then pairs of tokens are iteratively merged into bigger tokens according to a list of
merge rules

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{M}}
. We refer the reader to
Appendix

B
for a formal definition of BPE.

2.6
Canonicality

To facilitate discussion, we start by introducing some terminology. The set of
canonical strings
:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}\left\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}})\mid{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}^{*}}\right\}

(4a)

The set of
noncanonical strings

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}}}\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
, and the set of
canonical prefixes
:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}\left\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\preceq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\right\}

(4b)

We say that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}
is
canonical
if
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
. We say
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}
is a
canonical prefix
if
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}}}
.
We define the
canonicalization function

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}\to{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
as
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}))
.
Note that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}\}
. We say that our language model
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}
is
canonical

if
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\notin{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\Longrightarrow p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})=0
, i.e.,
\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})=1
.

Membership tests.

We can test membership in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
via the
round-trip test
:
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\Longleftrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}
.
We note, however, that testing membership in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}}}
for general tokenizers is less straightforward.
6
6

6

When the tokenization function
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}
is implemented as a finite-state transducer, we may derive an efficient finite-state automaton that describes the prefix language of its outputs.

Membership tests for BPE.

Appendix

B
provides efficient algorithms for membership in these sets for BPE.

Lemma

3
shows that for BPE
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\!=\!{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}}}
, i.e., the round-trip test is a correct membership test for
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}}}
.
We also provide a very efficient incremental algorithm for performing these membership tests that exploits the properties of the BPE encoding procedure.
Figure

2
shows examples of canonicalized BPE strings.

3
Canonicality by Conditioning

Recall (
Equation

2
) that a
tokenized
language model defines a language model
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
over
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}^{*}}
. However, it is common practice to evaluate the probability of a character string
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}^{*}}
using the following
sub
-distribution over
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}^{*}}
during both likelihood-based training and evaluation:

\displaystyle p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}}^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}))

(5)

The problem with
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}}^{\prime}
is that
\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}^{*}}}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}}^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
can be
<1
, making it is an improper probability distribution over
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}^{*}}
.

Unfortunately, every token string—canonical or not—is assigned a nonzero probability under a softmax-normalized model; thus, modern language models allocate (typically small) probability to each and every noncanonical string. The leakage of mass to noncanonical strings is compounded by the fact that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
is an
exponentially sparse
subset of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}
.
7
7

7

More precisely, as a function of a length limit
N
, the fraction of canonical strings in the universal of token strings
\frac{|\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\colon|{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}|\leq N\}|}{|\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}\colon|{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}|\leq N\}|}
decreases exponentially quickly. This is evident as each character string has one canonical encoding, and exponentially many noncanonical encodings as a function of its length.

Our first approach to this problem defines a language model
g
that is the result of
probabilistic conditioning
on the event that the generated token string is in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
.

Definition 1
.

The
globally canonicalized language models

g_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}
and
g
are defined as the following probability distributions over
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}^{*}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}
, respectively:

\displaystyle g_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}g({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}))

(6a)

\displaystyle g({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Y}\sim p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}\end{subarray}}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Y}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\,\middle|\,{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Y}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\right]

(6b)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Y}\sim p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}\end{subarray}}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Y}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Y}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\right]}{\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Y}\sim p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}\end{subarray}}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Y}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\right]}

(6c)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z}\,p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})\mathds{1}\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\}

(6d)

where
Z
is the
canonicality rate
:

\displaystyle Z\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Y}\in p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}\end{subarray}}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}Y}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\right]

(6e)

Note that
g_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}})=\frac{1}{Z}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}))
, meaning that we may interpret the globally canonicalized model as renormalizing
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}}^{\prime}
. We note that the effect of conditioning the language model to generate only canonical token strings may dramatically change the conditional prefix distributions of the distribution. We will discuss the details of how to approximately and exactly generate samples from
g
.

3.1
Theoretical Guarantees for Conditioning

Why do we condition?

The conditional distribution
g
lets us preserve what matters: the relative probabilities of canonical strings, ensuring that we eliminate the wasteful mass allocated to noncanonical strings without distorting the existing mass on canonical strings.
8
8

8

More formally, the conditional distribution is the
unique
distribution
q
over
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
that maintains the relative probabilities of any pair
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
—that is,
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}),q({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})=p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}),q({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}})
. The only way to achieve this is to set
q({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})=\eta,p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})
for all
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
, where normalization fixes
\eta=1/Z
. Thus,
q=g
is the unique way to preserve relative probability subject to the canonicality constraint.

The following proposition shows that test-time conditioning improves our estimate
g
of the true distribution
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}^{\star}
.

Proposition 1
.

Assuming that the true distribution over tokens
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}^{\star}
is canonical, the globally canonicalized model
g
guarantees the following reduction in KL divergence:

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}(p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}^{\star}\,\|\,p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}})-\mathrm{KL}(p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}^{\star}\,\|\,g)=\underbrace{-\log Z}_{\geq 0}

(7)

Proof: See
.

We may interpret
Proposition

1
as saying that, no matter how good or how bad of a model the original language model
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}
is with respect to the true distribution
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}^{\star}
, we can expect a reduction in the KL divergence equal to
-\log Z
, bringing it
closer
to
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}^{\star}
. This reduction is large if
Z
is small, i.e., the token strings generated in
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}
are unlikely to be canonical, modest if
Z\approx 1
, and zero if
Z=1
. In other words, the change in KL quantifies the value of having the constraint as prior knowledge.

3.2
Algorithms for Approximate Conditioning

3.2.1
Exact Algorithms

The simplest exact conditioning algorithm for sampling from
g
is
rejection sampling
:

⬇

def

rejection_sampling@$
()
$@
:

while

True
:

@$
\
tokseq

\
sim

\
pTokens$@

if

@$
\
tokseq

\
in

\
D$@
:

return

@$
\
tokseq$@

However, the rejection sampling algorithm can be inefficient if
Z
is small, as its expected running time per sample is
\mathcal{O}(1/Z)
. When
Z\approx 1
, this is pretty fast. However, when it is smaller, it can be unusably slow.

Another correct sampling algorithm is
ancestral sampling
:

⬇

def

ancestral_sampling@$
()
$@
:

@$
\
tokseq

\
gets

\
varepsilon$@

while

True
:

@$
\
tokenp

\
sim

\
prefixTokensGlobal
(

\
cdot

\
mid

\
tokseq
)
$@

if

@$
\
tokenp

=

\
eos$@
:

return

@$
\
tokseq$@

@$
\
tokseq

\
gets

\
tokseq

\
c

\
tokenp$@

Here, each step of the algorithm samples a token (or
eos
) from the globally canonicalized model’s conditional prefix distribution. Unfortunately, computing
\overrightarrow{g}
exactly is intractable as it requires summing over infinitely many future strings. Thus,
\overrightarrow{g}
must be approximated.
9
9

9

We also mention more sophisticated approximations, such as learning
(
Zhao et al. 2024
)
, adaptive upper bounds
(
Park et al. 2025
)
, and sequential Monte Carlo steering
(
Lew et al. 2023
;
Loula et al. 2025
)
. We do not explore these more sophisticated strategies, as the local approximation works surprisingly well.
In
Section

3.2.2
, we will provide a cheap, local approximation and, in
Section

3.2.3
, we will devise a strategy for improving the short-sightedness of the local approximation by using more computation.

3.2.2
A Locally Canonicalized Approximation

Our locally canonicalized model
\ell
is a distribution over
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}
that approximates
ancestral_sampling
for sampling from
g
by using the following local approximation
\overrightarrow{\ell}
to the global prefix probability
\overrightarrow{g}
.

Definition 2
.

The
locally canonicalized model

\ell
is a language model over
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}
, which assigns probability zero to noncanonical strings. The probability of a token string under
\ell
is defined auto-regressively as

\displaystyle\ell({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}\overrightarrow{\ell}({\textsc{eos}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})\prod_{t=1}^{|{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}|}\overrightarrow{\ell}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{t}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{<t})

(9a)

where its next-token conditional distributions are defined as

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\ell}({\textsc{eos}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}\frac{{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}}}({\textsc{eos}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})\mathds{1}\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\}}{\overrightarrow{\ell}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})}

(9b)

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\ell}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}\frac{{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})\,\mathds{1}\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}}}\}}{\overrightarrow{\ell}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})}

(9c)

where
\overrightarrow{\ell}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})
normalizes the distribution over
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}\cup\{{\textsc{eos}}\}
that lead to a prefix-canonical extension of the string:

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\ell}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}\mathds{1}\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\}\,{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}}}({\textsc{eos}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})

(9d)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}{\overrightarrow{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})\,\mathds{1}\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}}}\}

This distribution is practical for the following reasons:

1.

It is efficient: The canonicality checks are cheap to compute (
Appendix

B
), and there is very little additional overhead over sampling from the base conditional distribution.

2.

It has the correct support: The local distribution can only place positive probability on canonical strings; it eliminates noncanonical strings by masking out any token
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}
that would render the extended string
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}
noncanonical.

3.

It often gives a reasonable approximation to the conditional global distribution: We expect this to be a good approximation when
\overrightarrow{\ell}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})\approx 1
, which we may expect in practice as noncanonical strings tend to be assigned tiny probabilities, making the sum in
Equation

9d
close to
1
.

Unfortunately, the locally canonicalized model
\ell
gives a
warped
approximation to
g
, as
Proposition

2
shows:

Proposition 2
.

\displaystyle\ell({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})

\displaystyle=p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})\,\mathds{1}\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\}\Biggr[\underbrace{\prod_{t=1}^{|{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}|+1}\overrightarrow{\ell}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{<t})}_{\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.5382pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}{w_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})}\Biggr]^{-1}

(10a)

Thus,

\displaystyle{w_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})

\displaystyle=\frac{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})}{\ell({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})}\mathds{1}\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\}

(10b)

Proof: See
.

We call
{w_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})
the
weight
of the locally canonicalized sample (in relation to
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}^{\star}
). We note that the weight describes the change in relative probability of the canonical token strings
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
. We call this unwanted change
warping
. There is no warping in the globally canonicalized case, as the relative probability is unchanged. In
Section

3.2.3
, we describe methods that can
un
warp the local distribution at the cost of additional computation.

Despite the warping, the local distribution
\ell
comes with the following reassuring theoretical property, guaranteeing that it helps estimate the true distribution
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}^{\star}
.

Proposition 3
.

Assuming that the true distribution over tokens
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}^{\star}
is canonical, the locally canonicalized model
\ell
guarantees the following reduction in KL divergence:

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}(p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}^{\star}\,\|\,p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}})-\mathrm{KL}(p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}^{\star}\,\|\,\ell)=\underbrace{-\!\!\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\sim p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}^{\star}\end{subarray}}\left[\log{w_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})\right]}_{\geq 0}

(11)

Proof: See
.

Proposition

3
confirms the intuitive notion that local canonicalization cannot make the language model
worse
.
10
10

10

Proposition

3
may be generalized to any distribution
q
. Specifically,
\mathrm{KL}(p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}^{\star}\,\|\,p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}})-\mathrm{KL}(p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}^{\star}\,\|\,q)=-\!\!\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\sim p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}^{\star}\end{subarray}}\left[\log\frac{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})}{q({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})}\right]
. However, it may not necessarily be a
reduction
, as it is for
\ell
, if the
q
distorts the distribution too much.

3.2.3
Sampling-Based Inference

Unfortunately, sampling from
g
is hard because we cannot efficiently compute the conditional prefix probability
\overrightarrow{g}
(and rejection sampling is slow when
Z
is small). In this section, we show how to use the local approximation in conjunction with sampling-based inference to improve the fidelity to
g
at the cost of increased computation. These methods use the local distributions as a
proposal
distribution to produce candidates that will be properly weighted and resampled in a manner so that they maintain a principled approximation to the global distribution
g
. Therefore, in expectation and in the limit, these methods produce exact samples; thus, they do not warp the distribution.

The warping in the locally canonicalized method can occur because the sampling algorithm approximated the conditional prefix probability canonicality, meaning that we may sample a string of tokens that looks good initially, but we end up stuck with a bad string prefix because we overestimated the conditional prefix probability. In contrast, rejection sampling
restarts
the sample once noncanonicality is detected, whereas the local method
ignores
noncanonical tokens and
forces
the current string to complete. We can fix the bias in the local approximation by exploiting our knowledge of
{w_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})
for each sample
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\sim\ell
. More specifically,
{w_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})
is proportional to the over- or under-representation rate of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}
in samples from
\ell
with respect to the target distribution
g
. Therefore, we can correctly account for this
in expectation
by
weighting
each sample by its
{w_{\ell}}
factor. This is the principle behind the importance sampling methods we describe below.
11
11

11

For a rigorous treatment of the technical conditions required for importance resampling and estimation to converge, we refer the reader to
Chatterjee & Diaconis 2017
.

Proposition 4
.

\displaystyle Z=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\sim\ell\end{subarray}}\left[{w_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})\right]=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\sim\ell\end{subarray}}\left[\frac{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})\mathds{1}\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\}}{\ell({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})}\right]

(12)

Proof: See
.

Thus, we can apply
importance sampling
to obtain an unbiased estimate
\widehat{Z}
of
Z
given
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}}}^{(M)}\mathrel{\overset{\text{{{\scriptsize i.i.d.}}}}{\sim}}\ell
:

\displaystyle\widehat{Z}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}\frac{1}{M}\!\sum_{m=1}^{M}\!{w_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}}}^{(m)})

(13)

We may also estimate the global distribution
g
as follows:

\displaystyle\widehat{g}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}\frac{1}{M{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\widehat{Z}}\sum_{m=1}^{M}{w_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}}}^{(m)})\mathds{1}\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\!=\!{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}}}^{(m)}\}

(14)

Note that the quotient with
\widehat{Z}
leads to statistical bias; however, that bias vanishes as
M
grows larger.
If desired, we may also use
\widehat{g}
for drawing approximate samples using a method known as
importance resampling
. This method produces approximate samples from
g
by sampling
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\sim\widehat{g}
, which has a support set with size at most
M
.
Thus, importance sampling and resampling give us a parameter
M
that improves the approximation as we make it larger.
12
12

12

We note that for these estimates to converge to their correct values, we require
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})\mathds{1}\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\}>0\Longrightarrow\ell({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})>0
for all
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}
. This is ensured when our prefix canonicality test
\mathds{1}\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}}}\}
is
exact
. More generally, it is safe to allow for false positives but not false negatives.

4
Canonicality by Construction

The method we present in this section augments the output layer of the transformer architecture with canonicality constraints. Thus, it requires additional training and test-time computation. Fortunately, this additional computation, at least in the case of BPE, is minimal thanks to efficient algorithms for checking canonicality (see
Appendix

B
).

Suppose we have a
base architecture
, i.e., a parametric family of language models
\{p_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{D}}
that is parameterized by a real-valued vector of parameters
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
. We assume
\log p_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})
is continuously differentiable with respect to
\boldsymbol{\theta}
for all
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}
, and that each
p_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
is a valid language model over
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}
. For example,
\{p_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{D}}
can be a parametric family of transformers or recurrent neural networks.
13
13

13

Our experiments use transformer-based language models.
Let
\overrightarrow{p_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}
denote the conditional prefix probability of one such model.

Definition 3
.

Our
canonicalized architecture
is a parametric family of language models
\{\ell_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{D}}
that is built on top of a base architecture
\{p_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{D}}
. We define each
\ell_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
in terms of
p_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
in the following manner:

\displaystyle\ell_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}\overrightarrow{\ell_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}({\textsc{eos}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})\prod_{t=1}^{|{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}|}\overrightarrow{\ell_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{t}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{<t})

(15a)

where each
\overrightarrow{\ell_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(\cdot\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{<t})
is defined as one of the following distributions over
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}\cup\{{\textsc{eos}}\}
:

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\ell_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}\frac{\overrightarrow{p_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})\,\mathds{1}\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}}}\}}{\overrightarrow{\ell_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})}

(15b)

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\ell_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}({\textsc{eos}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}\frac{\overrightarrow{p_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}({\textsc{eos}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})\,\mathds{1}\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\}}{\overrightarrow{\ell_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})}

(15c)

and
\overrightarrow{\ell_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})
ensures normalization:

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\ell_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}\overrightarrow{p_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}({\textsc{eos}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})\,\mathds{1}\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\}

(15d)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime\prime}}}\overrightarrow{p_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime\prime}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})\,\mathds{1}\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}}}\}

Much like the locally canonicalized model (
Definition

2
), noncanonical strings are forced to have probability zero using canonicality checks in the definition of its conditional prefix probabilities
Equations

15b
and
15c
.

Why train?

Suppose the original model
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}
is equal to some
p_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
in our family; let
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(0)}
denote its parameters. Clearly, if we set
\boldsymbol{\theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(0)}
, the model
\ell_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
is no different from
\ell
. The reason we optimize
\boldsymbol{\theta}
beyond
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(0)}
is that the training objective for
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(0)}
pressured the parameters to model canonicality, but now that pressure is gone because the canonicalized architecture (
Definition

3
) enforces that constraint for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}
.
Thus, the parameters that were previously used to model canonicality preferences can be repurposed to model any other textual phenomena in the training data.

Training
p_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
.

Given a training corpus
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(M)}\mathrel{\overset{\text{{{\scriptsize i.i.d.}}}}{\sim}}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}}^{\star}
of character strings, let
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}}}^{(M)}
be their corresponding (canonical) tokenizations. We define
\mathcal{L}(p)
, the
log-loss
(average negative log-likelihood) of the training corpus under a language model
p
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(p)\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}-\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\log p({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}}}^{(m)})

(16)

Log-loss is a reasonable (and common) training objective, as minimizing
\mathcal{L}(\ell_{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
also minimizes
\mathrm{KL}(p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}^{\star}\|\ell_{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
in the limit of infinite data (with perfect optimization). However, training a large language model with
\mathcal{L}
from scratch is prohibitively costly. Instead, we fine-tune an existing (noncanonical) large language model.

We use the following
fine-tuning objective
, which strikes a balance between fitting the log-loss objective while maintaining some fidelity to the original language model
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}(1-\lambda)\cdot\mathcal{L}(\ell_{\boldsymbol{\theta}})+\lambda\cdot\mathrm{KL}(\ell_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\|p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}})

(17)

Here, we use the KL divergence between
\ell_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
and the original unconstrained model distribution
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}
as a regularizer
(
Christiano et al. 2017
;
Stiennon et al. 2020
;
Ziegler et al. 2020
;
Korbak et al. 2022
, e.g.,)
. The
regularization parameter

0\leq\lambda\leq 1
is used to trade fidelity to the original model (higher) against fidelity to the fine-tuning data (lower).

Optimization.

Our optimization algorithm is based on stochastic gradient descent. To optimize
\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
, on each step, we randomly choose with probability
\lambda
which of the two terms to perform a gradient step on.

1.

For the log-loss updates, we sample a minibatch of examples from the fine-tuning corpus and use the sample average of that minibatch’s gradient as an efficient approximation to the gradient over the entire corpus.

2.

We estimate the gradient of the KL regularization term using
Amini et al. 2025
’s
(
Amini et al. 2025
)
Rao–Blackwellized method using samples from the current
\ell_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
.

Experiments initialize
\ell_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
with the same parameters as
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}
.

5
Experiments

This section evaluates our proposed methods—
canonicality by constraints
(global and local;
Section

3
) and
canonicality by conditioning
(
Section

4
)—by measuring their impact on real datasets and language models. This evaluation complements the theoretical guarantees of
Proposition

1
and
Proposition

3
, by quantifying the log-loss (
\mathcal{L}
;
Equation

16
) on held-out data.

5.1
Setup

Datasets.

We experiment on the following two datasets:

•

Penn Treebank

(
Marcus et al. 1993
,
PTB
,)
(test split;
3761
strings,
82
k words,
439
k characters)

•

WikiText

(
Merity et al. 2017
)
(test split;
4358
strings,
234
k words and
1286
k characters)

Models.

We experiment with the following models:

•

GPT-2 models

(
Radford et al. 2019
)
of increasing size (small, medium, and large)

•

Llama models

(
Llama Team 2024
)
of increasing size (
3.2-1B
,
3.2-3B
, and
3.1-8B
).

Pre-tokenization.

Our efficient bigram-based canonicality test
\mathds{1}\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}}}\}
(
Appendix

B
) makes the simplifying assumption that the tokenizer
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}
is only based on BPE. However, in practice, systems use an additional
pre-tokenization
step that breaks text into chunks called pre-tokens (e.g., based on spaces and punctuation). Most pre-tokenizers are based on hand-crafted regular expressions; the GPT-2 and Llama models each use a distinct pre-tokenization regular expressions.
Since these models make use pre-tokenization, our incremental test occasionally makes some mistakes. Particularly, concerning are instances of
false negatives
, i.e., we rule out a bigram as noncanonical when it is in fact canonical. False negatives are problematic for estimation (
Footnote

12
).
These errors are caused by the interaction of the pre-tokenizer and the tokenizer.
14
14

14

For example, under the GPT-2 tokenizer, the token bigram
\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{\mbox{\n}}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{198}}}}}$}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\,\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{\mbox{\n}}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{198}}}}}$}}
may or may not be canonical, depending on the context.
For instance, in the following example, it is canonical:

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{\small\mbox{Hi,\n\nI}}})=\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{Hi}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{17250}}}}}$}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\,\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{,}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{11}}}}}$}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\,\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{\mbox{\n}}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{198}}}}}$}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\,\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{\mbox{\n}}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{198}}}}}$}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\,\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{I}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{40}}}}}$}}

because the pre-tokenizer creates separate pretokens for each newline, preventing BPE from merging them. However, in the next example, it is not canonical:

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{\small\mbox{Hi,\n\n}}})=\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{Hi}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{17250}}}}}$}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\,\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{,}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{11}}}}}$}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\,\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{\mbox{\n\n}}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{628}}}}}$}}

because the pre-tokenizer does not create separate pretokens for the newlines, allowing BPE to merge them.

To work around these rare exceptions, we determined a (small) set of
overrides
for our canonicality test by identifying the bigrams where we have made a false negative judgment on a corpus of canonical strings. We note that this workaround occasionally introduces false positives.
15
15

15

A complete solution to the pre-tokenization challenges would build a transducer that accurately models the pre-tokenizer, which we then compose with a transducer implementing BPE (see
). We leave this investigation for future work.

5.2
Canonicality by Conditioning

Methology.

Figure

3
reports the log-loss of the local
\ell
and global
g
methods on each dataset (
\{p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}^{(m)}\}_{m=1}^{M}
) as well as
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}
, which serves as our baseline. Below are the details of how we estimated the log-loss of each method.

•

Baseline
: The log-loss for the baseline method is

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\log p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{(m)})

(18a)

•

Global
: The log-loss for the global method is

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(g)=\mathcal{L}(p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}})+\log Z\approx\mathcal{L}(p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}})+\log\widehat{Z}

(18b)

where
\widehat{Z}
is computed by
Equation

13
using
2000
samples from
\ell
.
Note that
\log\widehat{Z}\leq 0
; thus, the global method can only improve the log-loss,
\mathcal{L}(g)\leq\mathcal{L}(p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}})
.
16
16

16

In the case of the GPT-2 models, we restricted strings to have length
\leq 1024
, as these models tend to ramble. We analyze this choice further in
Figure

4
.

•

Local
: The log-loss for the local method is

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\ell)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}(p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}})+\underbrace{\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\log{w_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{(m)})}_{\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.5382pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}\widehat{W}}

(18c)

Note that
\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\log{w_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{(m)})\leq 0
; thus, the local method can only improve the log-loss,
\mathcal{L}(\ell)\leq\mathcal{L}(p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}})
.

Relationship to theory.

Proposition

1
shows that the reduction in KL divergence is equal to
-\log Z
, which is equal to the difference in log-loss
\mathcal{L}(p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}})-\mathcal{L}(g)
.
Proposition

3
shows that the reduction in KL divergence is equal to
-\!\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\sim p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}^{\star}\end{subarray}}\left[\log{w_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})\right]
, which is equal to the difference in log-loss
\mathcal{L}(p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}})-\mathcal{L}(\ell)
(in expectation).

; bits/string) for the baseline (

Observations.

•

For the global method, the change in the log-loss is
independent
of the dataset because
\log\widehat{Z}
is
independent
of the dataset. However, for the local method, it is
dependent
because
\widehat{W}
is
dependent
on the dataset.

•

Statistical significance:

–

For the global method, the log-loss reduction is
positive
and
constant
for every string; therefore, it is trivially statistically significantly better than the baseline method according to the paired-permutation test (i.e.,
p=0
).

–

For the local method, the log-loss reduction is
positive
for all strings, but the amount
varies
across strings; nonetheless, it is trivially statistically significantly better than the baseline method according to the paired-permutation test (i.e.,
p=0
).

•

Local vs. global: On the PTB dataset, we found that the global method is significantly better than the local method for all models. However, on the WikiText dataset, the local model is better in all cases except GPT-2-medium.

Takeways.

On principle (
Section

3.1
), we maintain that the global method (based on conditioning) is the correct solution, as it is the only distribution that preserves the relative probabilities for canonical strings. Additionally, our experiments support the local method as a practical approximation to the global distribution, which we expected to be the case because the probability allocated to each noncanonical token is generally quite small, meaning that the warping is not large; see discussion
Item

3
in
Section

3.2.2
). Interestingly, on some datasets (e.g., WikiText), the warping induced by the local method with respect to the global distribution can be advantageous, i.e., its reduction in log-loss with respect to the WikiText distribution is larger than that of the global method.

Figure 4:
Log-canonicality rate vs. (tokenized) length, including 95% confidence intervals, for each model.

Log-canonicality rate vs. length.

We found that the GPT-2 models generate very long strings, so we investigated the tradeoffs in the log-canonicality rate for truncating the lengths of the strings generated by each model.
Figure

4
shows the effect of length on the log-canonicality rate estimate
\log\widehat{Z}
, using 2000 samples taken from
\ell
. We see that for the Llama methods, the log-canonicality rate stabilizes after
256
tokens, but for the GPT-2 models, it continues decreasing. Thus, for practical reasons, our experiments with GPT-2 will be based on a limit length of
1024
, but bear in mind that this means that the actual log-canonicality rate is likely to be much smaller, meaning the reduction in log-loss for GPT-2 is larger than
Figure

3
indicates.

Supplementary error analysis.

analyzes the most frequent canonicality errors made by each model.

5.3
Canonicality by Construction

Methodology.

We fine-tuned two language models, GPT-2
S
and GPT-2
M
,
17
17

17

Unfortunately, we are unable to fine-tune larger models due to computational constraints. However, the models used here serve as a proof of concept.
on the PTB train set and a subset of the WikiText train set with 50K strings and 4.2M words.
We consider fine-tuning the canonicalized architecture (
\ell_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
) and the original architecture (
p_{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}}
) using the training criterion
\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}
for
\lambda\in\{0.001,0.01,0.1,0.2\}
.
18
18

18

Each model is trained for
3
epochs using the AdamW optimizer
(
Loshchilov & Hutter 2019
)
with a learning rate of
5\mathrm{e}^{-5}
and linear learning rate decay. For efficiency, we use
bfloat16
to represent the model parameters. We use a minibatch of size
8
for estimating the gradient of each term of the
\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}
objective.

As a baseline, we consider the original model
p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}
and the locally constrained model
\ell
, as these models serve as initialization for fine tuning.

Figure

5
reports the log-loss
\mathcal{L}
(bits/string) on the held-out datasets for each method mentioned above.

Log-loss (bits/string) of fine-tuned models on held-out portions of WikiText and PTB. The column labeled N/A reports the log-loss of the baselines

Observations.

•

In all cases (i.e., models, datasets, and methods), we observe a large improvement in log-loss from fine-tuning. The reduction in log-loss is larger in the case of PTB.

•

Provided that
\lambda
is small enough, we see a consistent reduction in log-loss when comparing
\ell_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
and
p_{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}}
. However, the difference between
\ell_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
and
p_{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}}
is comparatively smaller than we saw in
Figure

3
.

•

Generally, a the smaller values of
\lambda
work best, indicating that reguarlization towards the original model should not be done too strongly.

Takeaways.

Unsurprisingly, fine-tuning for the specific dataset is useful regardless of whether we use the original or canonicalized architecture. Fine-tuning with the canonicalized architecture performs slightly better, but improvements appear to be small.
It is possible that training the canonicalized architecture from scratch on a huge dataset would yield better results than our proof-of-concept experiment.

Conclusion

We have demonstrated that enforcing canonicality in token-level language models eliminates systemic probability mass misallocation, leading to improved likelihoods on held-out data across multiple models and corpora. Our proposed methods—canonicality by conditioning and canonicality by construction—provide practical solutions that either refine inference or modify the model architecture to ensure only canonical token strings are assigned positive probability. In addition to the empirical benefits, our theoretical results establish that correcting these mistakes strictly improves model fit. Moreover, our discovery of an efficient incremental test for BPE canonicality simplifies prior approaches, making it more accessible for practical deployment. These findings underscore the importance of aligning token-based probability models with their training distributions, thereby paving the way for more accurate language modeling.

Impact Statement

Potential benefits.

By introducing methods to enforce canonicality, we improve a given model’s fidelity to the underlying distribution that generated the text. These contributions have broad implications for the reliability of language models, particularly in applications where precise probability estimates are crucial. By eliminating avoidable modeling errors, we advance the goal of more reliable and robust language models.

Potential harms.

Constraining tokenization may inadvertently reduce robustness to rare, noisy, or adversarial inputs by ruling out alternative tokenizations of the same character string. While the canonicality constraint does not remove any character strings from the language, it does restrict the model to assign nonzero probability only to a single canonical tokenization of each string. This may disproportionately affect rare or unconventional spellings, as their probability mass may be less sharply concentrated on a single tokenization. Future work should provide a deeper analysis of whether or not canonicality enforcement helps or hurts in these settings to ensure fair and robust language model behavior across diverse linguistic contexts.

Appendix A
Limitations and Future Work

Future experimental work.

A limitation of our experimental design is that we only evaluate our approach’s ability to estimate probabilities. We do not have any experiments evaluating any qualitative difference in the sample quality from the canonicalized methods.

Models overregularize.

Language models often place a higher probability on a noncanonical encoding of the string than the canonical one. Here is an interesting example from WikiText corpus

)
