UTF-8 Encoding.

The very first step in representing text is the choice of string encoding. UTF-8 is an encoding that maps strings of characters into bytes, which is used to store almost every webpage on the internet. The scheme uses a variable-width encoding of each Unicode character that uses between 1 and 4 bytes, with more common Unicode characters requiring fewer bytes. The UTF-8 encoding imposes additional validity constraints on token strings. In future work, we may extend our work on BPE encoding validity one step further to ensure that the byte string that the BPE string decodes is a valid UTF-8 string. For better or worse, UTF-8 decoding errors are typically suppressed, e.g., by the Huggingface transformers library.

Implementation note.

Byte-pair encoding (as the name suggests) typically takes
bytes
as inputs. Note that popular interfaces like huggingface try to make things easier for users by hiding the UTF-8 encoding step that maps strings to bytes from users by exposing only a string interface. We require finer-grained control to implement the round-trip test correctly; we must avoid encoding and decoding from the text’s encoding (e.g., UTF-8). Therefore, we use a custom implementation of byte-pair encoding. Note that the
transformers
library does not provide methods to work directly with byte strings.

"
".encode("utf-8")

= b’\xf0\x9f\xa4\x97’} is the encoding of the hugging face emoji \includegraphics[height=1em]
twitter/huggingface-LaTeX.png.

However, if we try to decode the following prefix of the byte string
b’\xf0\x9f\xa4’.decode("utf-8")}, we get an error as it is an invalid UTF-8 string. Note that if we use \mintinline
pythonb’
\xf
0
\x
9f
\xa
4’.decode("utf-8", errors="ignore") or
b’\xf0\x9f\xa4’.decode("utf-8", "replace")} the errors may go unnoticed. Many packages (e.g., \texttt
transformers and
tiktoken
) suppress these errors.

Other tokenization models.

We limited our experiments to BPE, as it is by far the most commonly used tokenization model (
Footnote

5
). However, many other tokenizers exist, such as longest-match tokenization (used by WordPiece) and other finite-state transduction-based tokenizers. These other tokenizers can plug directly into our framework as long as they provide efficient membership tests for
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}}}
. In the case of finite-state-transduction methods (which include longest-match tokenizers), the membership is a straightforward operation on the automaton. We note that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}}}
, in general—and, it specifically does not hold for longest-match tokenizers.

More sophisticated inference algorithms.

Future work may wish to explore
sequential
importance resampling (SIR) methods or other sequential Monte Carlo methods (SMC), which have shown great promise as methods for constrained generation from language models
(
Lew et al. 2023
;
Zhao et al. 2024
, e.g.,)
. Sequential importance resampling, for example, provides the computational benefit of not waiting until a complete string is sampled from the local model to resample. It works by evolving many string prefixes in parallel. Now, when any given string is “stuck” in a low-probability region, it can be probabilistically replaced by another string in the collection. Many SMC algorithms, including SIR, are GPU-friendly.

Distillation.

Another interesting direction to explore related to fine-tuning is the distillation of the (approximately) globally canonicalized model
g
. We could do that by minimizing
\mathrm{KL}(g\|\ell_{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
using stochastic gradient descent.

Precision vs. recall.

It can be challenging to correctly capture all of the constraints that the inputs must satisfy. In this paper, we explored the canonicality constraints that BPE models have. We provided efficient methods for testing canonicality, and we needed to validate those against reference canonicality procedures (as a kind of regression test). It may be impossible to capture every nuance of a particular implementation perfectly. And, in some cases, there are even “bugs” or “undesirable” behaviors that the reference implementations have. For example, the pre-tokenization system used by GPT-2 was designed to split the possessive marker (
‘s
) from a phrase like
OpenAI‘s␣system

\to

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\texttt{OpenAI}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}|}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\texttt{`s}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}|}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\texttt{{\textvisiblespace}system}}
; however, the rule has the following behavior which is likely not intensional

OpenAI␣Inc.‘s␣system

\to

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\texttt{OpenAI}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}|}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\texttt{{\textvisiblespace}Inc}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}|}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\texttt{.`}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}|}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\texttt{s}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}|}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\texttt{{\textvisiblespace}system}}
, as it fails to split the possessive correctly because the regular expression used by the pre-tokenizer prioritizes
.‘
over
‘s
in this example.
More generally, reasoning about a complicated software system using formal models will always present a challenge between fidelity and analytical tractability.

Evaluation on downstream tasks.

Our study deliberately centers on a task-agnostic metric—KL divergence. We proved that our canonicalization methods are guaranteed to improve this metric (
Propositions

1
and
3
) and we quantified the effect empirically (
Section

5
). However, many language-model applications are judged with task-specific metrics that may be
misaligned
with KL divergence. Thus, an improvement in KL divergence does not guarantee an improvement in the task-specific metrics. Assessing how canonicalization influences concrete reasoning tasks—under each task’s own evaluation metric—remains an important direction for future work.

Appendix B
Efficient Membership Tests for BPE

This section gives a formal definition of byte-pair encoding (BPE) and novel algorithms for the following membership tests pertaining to it:

•

the canonical set
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
(see
Theorem

1
)

•

the canonical prefix set
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}}}
(see
Lemma

3
)

•

an incremental membership test for single-token extensions, i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}}}
given that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}}}
(see
Proposition

5
)

Our key insight is that a token string is canonical if and only if all of its constituent bigrams are canonical; see
Theorem

1
. Thus, our membership test runs in time linear in the length of the string—we iterate over all bigrams in the strings and check their canonicality; we call this the
bigram test
. Moreover, our test is simple to implement, unlike similar tests given in prior work
(
Berglund et al. 2024
;
Cognetta & Okazaki 2024
, e.g.,)
. A consequence of
Theorem

1
is that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}}}
(see
Lemma

3
), which means that membership in the set of canonical prefixes reduces to membership in the canonical set.
19
19

19

We note that, in principle, the bigram canonicality tests can all be precomputed and cached for maximal efficiency. However, we found that to be impractical for models with large token vocabularies. In
Section

B.3
, we provide a very efficient method that can test bigram canonicality.

B.1
Byte-Pair Encoding

Byte-pair encoding
(
Gage 1994
;
Sennrich et al. 2016
, BPE;)
is currently the most commonly used tokenization method, as it has been used in most recent state-of-the-art language models.
20
20

20

See
Footnote

5
.

We formalize BPE as follows.

Definition 4
.

Byte-pair encoding
is a pair
({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{M}})
that defines a tokenization model
({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa})
. We describe the assumptions on
({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{M}})
and the construction of
({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa})
below:

•

{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}
is a
base alphabet
of characters (or bytes)

•

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{M}}=\left[\langle{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{(1)}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{(1)}^{\prime\prime}}\rangle,\ldots,\langle{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{(M)}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{(M)}^{\prime\prime}}\rangle\right]
is the
merge list
where
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{(m)}^{\prime}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{(m)}^{\prime\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}^{+}
for
m=1,\ldots,M
.

•

We define the
subword alphabet

{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\underline{\mathrm{S}}}\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\prime\prime}}\mid\langle{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\prime\prime}}\rangle\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{M}}\}
.

•

We define the
token alphabet

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}
such that for each
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}\ \in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}
, there is a unique object (e.g., an identifier) for each distinct element of the subword alphabet
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\underline{\mathrm{S}}}
. The reason for this technical detail is to avoid confusion between the concatenation of subwords and the concatenation of tokens.

•

We define the
base encoder

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\underline{\mathrm{S}}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}}
and
base decoder

{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}}\to{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\underline{\mathrm{S}}}
as bijective maps between the subword alphabet
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\underline{\mathrm{S}}}
and a set of
base tokens

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}
.

•

We define the
decoder

{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}\to{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}^{*}}
to be the pointwise extension of the base decoder:
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{1}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{N})\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{1})\cdots{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{N})
.

•

We define the
encoder

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}^{*}}\to{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}
in the pseudocode, which is a deterministic
string rewriting process
based on applying a sequence of merge rules to the current string of tokens until no more merges are available.
21
21

21

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}
can be implemented much more efficiently using a careful choice of data structure
(
Zouhar et al. 2023
, see, e.g.,)
.

22
22

22

n
+2}

\
cdots

\
token_N$@

return

@$
\
token_1

\
cdots

\
token_N$@

Note that the merge rules are applied in a specific deterministic order to any given string; thus, there is a unique way to
derive
the output tokenization from the input string.

B.2
Simple and Efficient Membership Tests

Definition 5
.

We define the
set of canonical bigrams
as

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{B}}\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}\left\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}\right\}.

(19)

where
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}
is the canonicalization function (

).

Definition 6
.

We define the
set of bigram-canonical strings

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}B}
as strings composed of only canonical bigrams:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}B}\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}\colon\textsc{bigrams}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{B}}\}.

(20)

In general, it is possible for a token in the token vocabulary not to be canonical. Such tokens can always be removed from the token vocabulary without changing the result of the BPE tokenizer. We assume, for simplicity, that the token vocabulary contains no such tokens.

Assumption 1
.

For all
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}
.

Remark 1
.

It is straightforward to verify that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{\infty}
, as it is equivalent to code for
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}(\cdot))
where we have added the option of truncating the number of iterations performed in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}
’s fixpoint iteration loop. Setting
T=\infty
, disables the truncation; thus,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{\infty}
.

Lemma 1

(Canonicalization process synchronization)
.

Suppose that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
or both
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
. Suppose further that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}
is nonempty.
Then, there exists a sequence
\{(a_{t},b_{t},c_{t},d_{t},e_{t})\}_{t=1}^{\infty}
such that the following equations hold for all
t\geq 0
:

\displaystyle P_{t}\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{t}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{a_{t}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}){\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{b_{t}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}){\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{c_{t}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}})\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{d_{t}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{a_{t}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}){\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{b_{t}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}})\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{e_{t}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{b_{t}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}){\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{c_{t}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}})\end{cases}

(21)

Proof.

We will prove
Lemma

1
by induction on
t
, and by explicitly constructing the sequence
\{(a_{t},b_{t},c_{t},d_{t},e_{t})\}_{t=1}^{\infty}
.

Let
T
denote the number of distinct iterations of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}})
.

Base case (
P_{0}
).
In this case, it is straightforward to verify that
(a_{0},b_{0},c_{0},d_{0},e_{0})=(0,0,0,0,0)
satisfies
P_{0}
because each canonicalization call returns precisely the base tokenization of its argument. Thus,
P_{0}
holds.

Induction step.

Suppose
P_{0},{\ldots},P_{j}
hold. We seek to show that
P_{j+1}
holds.

Since
P_{j}
holds, there must exist
(a_{t},b_{t},c_{t},d_{t},e_{t})
satisfying the equations in
P_{t}
. To prove that
P_{t+1}
holds, we will construct
(a_{t+1},b_{t+1},c_{t+1},d_{t+1},e_{t+1})
such that the equations in
P_{t+1}
.

We first observe that at time
t
,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{t}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}^{(t)}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}^{(t)}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}^{(t)}
where

•

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}^{(t)}
is the tokenization at step
t
of the substring over the characters in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}
in the canonicalization process for
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}

•

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}^{(t)}
is the tokenization at step
t
of the substring over the characters in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}
in the canonicalization process for
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}

•

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}^{(t)}
is the tokenization at step
t
of the substring over the characters in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}
in the canonicalization process for
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}

The only way for this observation to be false is for a merge to straddle the boundary between
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}^{(t)}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}^{(t)}
or
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}^{(t)}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}^{(t)}
, which is impossible because of our premise that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
or both
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
. More specifically, if there ever were a merge between

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}^{(t)}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}^{(t)}
then
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}
could not be canonical, as the final tokenization would not respect the boundary between
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}
. Similarly, if there ever were a merge between
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}^{(t)}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}^{(t)}
, then by analogous reasoning,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}
could not be canonical.

We now turn to case analysis:

1.

Suppose
t<T
, then the
(t\!+\!1)
th
step of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}})
applies to the highest-priority
23
23

23

I.e., the bigram with the lexicographically lowest pair of (position in the merge list, position in the current token string). Note that there can be no ties under this ordering.
bigram of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}^{(t)}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}^{(t)}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}^{(t)}
. Consider the following subcases characterizing the possible positions for this merge:

(a)

The merge is in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}^{(t)}
.

Then,

(a_{t+1},b_{t+1},c_{t+1},d_{t+1},e_{t+1})=(a_{t}+1,b_{t},c_{t},d_{t}+1,e_{t})

satisfies
P_{t+1}
because

•

It must also be the highest-priority merge in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}^{(t)}
,
which is step
a_{t+1}
of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}})
.

•

It must also be the highest-priority merge in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}^{(t)}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}^{(t)}
,
which is step
d_{t+1}
of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}})
.

•

The other canonicalization processes are unaffected, so they copy their position on this step.

(b)

The merge is in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}^{(t)}
.

Then,

(a_{t+1},b_{t+1},c_{t+1},d_{t+1},e_{t+1})=(a_{t},b_{t}+1,c_{t},d_{t}+1,e_{t}+1)

satisfies
P_{t+1}

because

•

It must also be the highest-priority merge in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}^{(t)}
,
which is step
b_{t+1}
of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}})
.

•

It must also be the highest-priority merge in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}^{(t)}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}^{(t)}
,
which is step
d_{t+1}
of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}})
.

•

It must also be the highest-priority merge in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}^{(t)}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}^{(t)}
,
which is step
e_{t+1}
of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}})
.

•

The other canonicalization processes are unaffected, so they copy their position on this step.

(c)

The merge is in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}^{(t)}
.

Then,

(a_{t+1},b_{t+1},c_{t+1},d_{t+1},e_{t+1})=(a_{t},b_{t},c_{t}+1,d_{t},e_{t}+1)

satisfies
P_{t+1}

because

•

It must also be the highest-priority merge in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}^{(t)}
,
which is step
c_{t+1}
of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}})
.

•

It must also be the highest-priority merge in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}^{(t)}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}^{(t)}
,
which is step
e_{t+1}
of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}})
.

•

The other canonicalization processes are unaffected, so they copy their position on this step.

2.

Suppose
t\geq T
. No merges exist.

Then,
(a_{t+1},b_{t+1},c_{t+1},d_{t+1},e_{t+1})=(a_{t},b_{t},c_{t},d_{t},e_{t})

satisfies
P_{t+1}
because
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{t}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{T}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}^{(t)}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}^{(t)}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}^{(t)}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}^{(T)}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}^{(T)}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}^{(T)}
, by definition of
T
, which implies that no further changes are possible and all processes must copy their position on this step, and continue to do so forever.

Therefore,
P_{t}
holds for all
t\geq 0
by the principle of induction.
∎

Example 1
.

This example seeks to illustrate
Lemma

1
. Consider the following three token strings from the GPT-2 tokenizer:

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}=\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{re}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{260}}}}}$}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\,\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{-}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{12}}}}}$}}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}=\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{token}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{30001}}}}}$}}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}=\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{ized}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{1143}}}}}$}}

For these tokens, we have
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}})={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\texttt{\small re-tokenized}}
. Our worked example will focus on only the first equation in
P_{t}
(repeated below for convenience), as the other two equations behave very similarly:

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{t}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{a_{t}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}){\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{b_{t}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}){\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{c_{t}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}})

Before moving forward with the example, we note that each token during the canonicalization process will be rendered as a tree because it is a convenient representation for showing which merges were done to build each of them.
24
24

24

We give a more formal presentation of these trees in
Section

B.3
.
For example, our inputs
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}
are represented by the following trees:

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}=\begin{forest}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}=\begin{forest}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}=\begin{forest}

Each row in the table below corresponds to a time step
t
, and the content of each column is the status of the canonicalization process at that time. We mark cells that are copied from the cell immediately above them with a dash (—).

t

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{t}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}})

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{a_{t}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}})

We see that at every step
t
, the equation
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{t}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{a_{t}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}){\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{b_{t}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}){\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{c_{t}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}})
is satisfied for an appropriate choice of
(a_{t},b_{t},c_{t})
. Specifically, we count the number of non-copy actions in each row. In the example, for
t=8
, we have
(a_{t},b_{t},c_{t})=(1,4,3)
. This example also allows us to see the case analysis used in the proof for the
location of the merge
in action.

Lemma 2

(Interlocking Canonicalization)
.

\displaystyle\forall{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{+},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\Longleftrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\land{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}

(22)

Proof.

Fix
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{+}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}
arbitrarily.

We will consider the direction of the bi-implication separately.

Case (
\Longrightarrow
)
. Suppose that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
. Then,
Lemma

1
gives us a sequence
\{(a_{t},b_{t},c_{t},d_{t},e_{t})\}_{t=1}^{\infty}
such that the following equations hold for all
t\geq 0
.

\displaystyle P_{t}\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{t}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{a_{l}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}){\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{b_{t}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}){\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{c_{t}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}})\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{d_{t}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{a_{t}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}){\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{b_{t}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}})\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{e_{t}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{b_{t}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}){\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{c_{t}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}})\end{cases}

(23a)

Now, consider
(a_{T},b_{T},c_{t},d_{T},e_{T})
, which gives

\displaystyle\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{T}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{a_{T}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}){\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{b_{T}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}){\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{c_{T}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}})\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{d_{T}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{a_{T}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}){\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{b_{T}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}})\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{e_{T}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{b_{T}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}){\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}_{c_{T}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}})\end{cases}

(23b)

which implies the following because, at time
T
,
each
of the canonicalization processes has reached its respective fixpoint:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}){\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}){\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}})\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}){\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}})\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}){\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}})\end{cases}

(23c)

which implies the following because each call to
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}
must return the canonicalization of its respective argument:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}\end{cases}

(23d)

Therefore,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}
are also canonical.

Case (
\Longleftarrow
)
.
Suppose
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
. Then, following an identical argument as the
\Longrightarrow
-case, we can see that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{c}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
.

Thus, the bi-implication holds; thus,
Lemma

2
holds.
∎

Theorem

1
establishes that a BPE token string is canonical if and only if all of its bigrams are canonical.
25
25

25

Note that if a string with length less than or equal to one has no bigrams; thus, the property is trivially satisfied, i.e., all length-zero and length-one token strings are canonical. The latter is thanks to
1
.

Theorem 1
.

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}B}
.

Proof.

We seek to prove, for all
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}
, that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\Longleftrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}B}
. We do this using induction on prefixes of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}
and
Lemma

2
.

Choose
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{1}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{N}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}
arbitrarily. Let the induction hypothesis
P(i)
be that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\leq i}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\Longleftrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\leq i}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}B}
.

Base cases.
We first prove
P(0)
and, if
|{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}|\geq 1
,
P(1)
.

•

Case
P(0)
.
To prove
P(0)
, we seek to show that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\leq 0}=\varepsilon\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\Longleftrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\leq 0}=\varepsilon\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}B}
. Both sides of the bi-implication are always true:
\varepsilon\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
because
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}(\varepsilon)={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}(\varepsilon))={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}(\varepsilon)=\varepsilon
; and
\varepsilon\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}B}
because it contains no bigrams, hence it is vacuously true. Therefore,
P(0)
is true.

•

Case
P(1)
.
To prove that
P(1)
is true for cases where
|{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}|\geq 1
, we seek to show that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\leq 1}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{1}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\Longleftrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\leq 1}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{1}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}B}
. Again, both sides of the bi-implication are always true. By
1
, we know that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{1}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
is true. Since
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{1}
has no bigrams,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{1}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}B}
is vacuously true. Therefore,
P(1)
is true.

Induction step.
Assume
P(i)
, that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\leq i}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\Longleftrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\leq i}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}B}
, where
i\geq 2
. We seek to prove
P(i+1)
, that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\leq i+1}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\Longleftrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\leq i+1}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}B}
. We prove each direction of the bi-implication separately.

•

Forward.
We seek to prove
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\leq i+1}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\leq i+1}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}B}
. Assume
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\leq i+1}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\leq i-1}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{i+1}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
. By
Lemma

2
, we know that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\leq i-1}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{i}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{i+1}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
. Since
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\leq i-1}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{i}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
, by
P(i)
, we know that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\leq i-1}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{i}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}B}
. Since
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\leq i-1}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{i}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}B}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{i+1}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
, all of the bigrams in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\leq i-1}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{i+1}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\leq i+1}
are canonical, so
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\leq i+1}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}B}
.

•

Backward.
We seek to prove
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\leq i+1}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}B}\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\leq i+1}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
. Assume
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\leq i+1}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\leq i-1}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{i+1}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}B}
. Then
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\leq i-1}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{i}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}B}
, because it contains only a subset of the bigrams in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\leq i-1}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{i+1}
. Then, by
P(i)
, we know that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\leq i-1}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{i}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
. From
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\leq i-1}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{i+1}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}B}
and
Definition

6
, we know that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{i+1}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
. Since
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\leq i-1}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{i}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{i+1}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
, by
Lemma

2
, we know that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\leq i-1}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{i+1}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\leq i+1}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
.

By induction, we know
P(N)
is true, so
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\Longleftrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}B}
, and the proof is complete.
∎

Lemma 3
.

For BPE,
26
26

26

More generally,
Lemma

3
holds for any tokenization model with a bigram-based canonicality test (i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\Longleftrightarrow\textsc{bigrams}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{B}}
.
the set of canonical strings is prefix closed, i.e.,

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}}}
.

Proof.

We prove
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
.

•

Part 1
(
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}}}
). Obvious by definition of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}}}
.

•

Part 2
(
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
).

1.

Suppose
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}}}

2.

\Longrightarrow
by definition of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}}}
, there is a
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\preceq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}

3.

\Longrightarrow
by
Theorem

1
, the bigrams of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}
are all canonical

4.

\Longrightarrow
since
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\preceq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}
, its bigrams are a subset of those of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}
, so they are also all canonical

5.

\Longrightarrow
since the bigrams of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}
are all canonical, by
Theorem

1
,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}

Therefore,
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}}}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}
.
∎

B.3
An
Even Faster
Bigram Test

BPE derivations.

Our canonicality test involves the inspection of the merge structure within a tokenization of a string. For that purpose, we define the
derivation
of a given string
{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\gamma}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}})}
as a string of trees
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}t}_{1}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}t}_{N}
where each
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}t}_{n}
(for
n=1,\ldots,N
) is a binary tree. For our purposes, a (binary)
tree
is a recursive data type composed of either a pair of trees or a base token. The pseudocode below shows how
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}
can be augmented to produce these derivation trees for a given string:

⬇

def

@$
\
canonicalTree
{\
chars_1

\
cdots

\
chars_N
}
$@
:

@$
\
Ds

\
gets

\
baseEncoder
(\
chars_1
)

\
cdots

\
baseEncoder
(\
chars_N
)
$@

while

True
:

@$
\
Ds
^\
prime

\
gets$@

step_derivation
(
@$
\
Ds$@
)

if

@$
\
Ds
^\
prime

=

\
Ds$@
:

return

@$
\
Ds$@

@$
\
Ds

\
gets

\
Ds
^\
prime$@

⬇

def

step_derivation
(
@$
\
ds
[1]

\
cdots

\
ds
[
N
]
$@
):

for

@$
\
Tuple
{\
charseqp
,

\
charseqpp
}

\
in

\
merges$@
:

for

@$n

\
in

[1,

\

-1]
$@
:

if

@$
\

{\

(\

[

]),

\

(\

[

=

\

{\

,

\

}
$@

@$
\

[1]

\

\

[

\

@$
\

[1]

\

[

$@

Note that
{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\gamma}(\cdot)}
is an adaptation of
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}
. The difference is that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}}}
has been replaced with a function that creates trees instead of replacing them with tokens. We have also extended
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}}}
to return the subword denoted by the derivation.

For each token
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}
, we associate with it a unique
canonical derivation

{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\gamma}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta})}
. However, we note that when
|{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\gamma}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta})}|>1
, it means that the token
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}
does not canonically tokenize to itself.
1
ensures
|{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\gamma}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta})}|=1
for all
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}
.

Additional notation.

For notational convenience, we define the following:

•

For any
\ell\!\in\!{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}^{+}\!\!\times\!{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}^{+}
, let
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{M}}[\ell]
denote the following:

–

If
\ell\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}
, return its position in some fixed, arbitrary ordering of
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}
.

–

If
\ell\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}^{+}\!\!\times\!{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}^{+}
, return the rank position of the merge
\ell
in the merge list plus
|{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}|
(as an offset), and
\infty
if
\ell
is not in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{M}}
.

•

We extend
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{M}}[{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}t}]
to trees
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}t}
as follows.

–

For a tree in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}t}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}}
:
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{M}}[{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}t}]\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{M}}[{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}t})]
.

–

For a tree of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}t}=\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s^{\prime}}\rangle
:
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{M}}[{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}t}]\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{M}}[\langle{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s}),{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s^{\prime}})\rangle]
.

•

We define the following
ordering relations
on merge rules and subtrees: We define
\ell<r\Longleftrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{M}}[\ell]<{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{M}}[r]
. We define
\ell>r
,
\ell\leq r
, and
\ell\geq r
analogously.

•

Given a token
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}
, we define the
left spine

\mathcal{S}({{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\gamma}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta})})
of
{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\gamma}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta})}
to be the ordered set containing the root node and its left descendants
27
27

27

We say that a node
p
is a
left descendant
of a node
q
if
p
is the left child of
q
or a left descendant of the left child of
q
. We define the right descendants of
p
in an analogous manner.
(ordered from root to leaf); and, we define the
right spine

\mathcal{R}({{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\gamma}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta})})
analogously (for right descendants).

•

In a given tree, we define
\pi
as the function that returns the parent node for any of its subtrees.

Intuition.

At a high level, we say that a token
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime\prime}}

conflicts
with a bigram
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}
if and only if
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime\prime}}
applies with an earlier rank over any overlapping span of the same character string. More formally, we say that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime\prime}}
(nontrivially)
overlaps
with
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}
if and only if

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\sigma}_{1}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\sigma}_{n}

(24a)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\sigma}_{n+1}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\sigma}_{N}

(24b)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\sigma}_{1}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\sigma}_{n}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\sigma}_{n+1}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\sigma}_{N}

(24c)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime\prime}})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\sigma}_{i}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\sigma}_{j}\quad\text{where }1\leq i\leq n<j\leq N

(24d)

However, for
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime\prime}}
to conflict with
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}
, it would need to include a merge over the span
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\sigma}_{i}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\sigma}_{j}
that blocks at least one merge in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}
from occurring in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}})
.

{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}\left(\hbox to157.67pt{\vbox to86.85pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.69249pt\lower-18.76355pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

In the diagram above, the derivation for the bigram
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}
is denoted by the juxtaposition of two triangles, one for each token’s derivation tree. We have denoted the canonical derivation as a trapezoid, as it is generally the union of multiple triangles; there may be 1, 2, or more tokens in the canonicalized bigram
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}
).
28
28

28

Please refer back to
Figure

2
for examples.
In orange, we have drawn a third triangle for the conflicting token
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime\prime}}
. We note that
{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\gamma}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime\prime}})}
must intersect
{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\gamma}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta})}
at a node
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s}
along its right-spine
\mathcal{R}({{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\gamma}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta})})
, and it must intersect
{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\gamma}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}})}
at a node
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s^{\prime}}
along its left-spine
\mathcal{S}({{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\gamma}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}})})
. In the diagram, the
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s^{\prime}}
represent the subtrees at respective points of the intersection with the hypothetical conflicting tree. The orange arc from
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s^{\prime}}
is used to show that any two nodes along the left and right edges could, in principle, be a conflicting tree. Crucially, however, the rank of the merge pair must precede the competing merge pairs present in the existing tokens
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}
. We make this more precise below.

Definition 8
.

A token
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime\prime}}

conflicts
with the bigram
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}
if and only if
{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\gamma}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime\prime}})}
is a subtree of
{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\gamma}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}))}
(which may be a string of trees) but not a subtree of either
{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\gamma}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta})}
or
{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\gamma}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}})}
.

We can find a conflicting token by exploiting the structure of the tokenization algorithm.

Definition 9
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime\prime}}
be a token such that
{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\gamma}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime\prime}})}=\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s^{\prime}}\rangle
for some
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s}\in\mathcal{R}({{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\gamma}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta})})
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{S}({{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\gamma}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}})})
. We say that
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime\prime}}
is a
minimal conflict
for the bigram
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}
if and only if
\pi({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s})>\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s^{\prime}}\rangle\leq\pi({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s^{\prime}})
.

The reason why
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime\prime}}
identified by
Definition

9
is
minimal
is that its definition identifies the earliest possible deviation, i.e., the earliest merge rule that could apply on their shared string
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\sigma}_{1}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\sigma}_{N}
that was not in the existing merges in
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}
. Note that the asymmetry
<
versus
\leq
is precisely what breaks ties between equal rank merges in favor of the left-most match, as prescribed in the procedure for
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}
that was provided in
Definition

4
.

Below is an algorithm for finding the minimal conflicting tree between
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}
, if one exists.

⬇

The
find_conflict
algorithm finds the minimal conflict between a pair of tokens
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}
(if one exists). We note that a useful consequence of
1
is that we do not need to check subtrees that exist off of the spines for conflicts, making our procedure more efficient than computing the canonicalized string from scratch, e.g., by running
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}
on the decoded string
{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}})
. Instead, our algorithm searches for a minimal conflicting tree: either it stops once it has found such a tree, or it has exhausted the limited options for such a tree. Finding the minimal conflict (if it exists) is
also
faster than computing the entire canonical tokenization
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}})
, as it only requires finding the first difference rather than completely building the canonicalized bigram.
29
29

29

We note that the
find_conflict
algorithm can be efficiently vectorized so that it computes the complete vector of valid next tokens very efficiently. We will provide a PyTorch implementation in our public code release (upon publication).

Definition 10
.

We define
\Phi\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}\times{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}
as the following relation on pairs of tokens:

\displaystyle\Phi({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{\tiny def}}}{=}}(\texttt{find\_conflict}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}})=\texttt{None})

(25)

The proposition below establishes that
\Phi
provides a correct membership test for the set of canonical bigrams
{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{B}}
.

Proposition 5
.

\Phi({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}})\Longleftrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{B}}

Proof.

Observe that
(\Phi({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}})\Longleftrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{B}})\equiv(\neg\Phi({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}})\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}\notin{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{B}})\land(\neg\Phi({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}})\Longleftarrow{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}\notin{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{B}})
. We will prove the proposition by proving each conjunct separately.

Part 1 (
\neg\Phi({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}})\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}\notin{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{B}}
).

1.

Suppose
\neg\Phi({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}})
. Then, there exists a conflicting pair
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s^{\prime}}\rangle=\mbox{\verb{}{}\lx@hidden@egroup find_conflict{}{}\lx@hidden@egroup find_conflict}(${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}$, ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}$)$,i.e.,$\langle${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s}$, ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s^{\prime}}$ \rangle$satisfies$$\pi$(${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s}$) > \langle${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s}$, ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s^{\prime}}$ \rangle \leq$\pi$(${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s^{\prime}}$)$.Belowisaschematicrepresentationofsuchaconflict:\vskip-12.0pt$$\hbox to269.87pt{\vbox to143.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 31.7934pt\lower-134.72415pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
