\par
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{equation*}This implies that ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s}$ must be a subtree along the right edge of ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s^{\prime}}$ must be a subtree along the left edge of ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}$, as those are precisely the only ways that the straddling merge can occur under our circumstances. Lastly, for ${\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s^{\prime}}}\rangle}$ to block ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}$, it would need to satisfy $\pi({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s})>{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s^{\prime}}}\rangle}\leq\pi({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}s^{\prime}})$.
\par\par\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@This characterization is equivalent to $\neg\Phi({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}})$.
\par\end{enumerate}\par\qed\end@proof\par\par\par\par\par\lx@newpage\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Connections to Canonicality Algorithms}
\par\begin{definition}We can describe the set of canonical tokenizations with the following finite-state automaton:
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@$\mathit{states}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}$
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@$\mathit{alphabet}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}$
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@$\mathit{transitions}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{B}}\}$
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@$\mathit{initial}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}$
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@$\mathit{accepting}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}$
\end{itemize}\end{definition}
\par Note that we never construct the automaton.
\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Generality.}
Our method works under a broader set of assumptions than \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{berglund2024constructing,lindmark2024analyzing}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}, as they require the merge list to be \emph{proper}. This condition is, in fact, restrictive: the Llama tokenizer (used in our experiments) is not proper, but the GPT-2 tokenizer is proper.
\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Practicality.}
The automaton has $V$ nodes and $\leq V^{2}$ edges where $V=|{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}|$. In practice, the machine is very close to having $V^{2}$ edges (even after DFA minimization!) because the transitions do not rule out many tokens. This means that the explicit machine is enormous. For example, GPT2 has 50K states and nearly 2,500,000,000 edges, making it way too large to build. Storing the edges that are \emph{absent is significantly more efficient}. On the other hand, our version never builds the machine, so the memory complexity is not a problem. It is also possible to use a more sophisticated automata representation that allows for \emph{failure transitions}; we refer the reader to \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{cognetta2024tokenizationfinitestatetransduction,lindmark2024analyzing}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} for further discussion of the technique.\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\lx@newpage\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@numbered@section{appendix}{toc}{Supporting Proofs and Lemmata}
\begin{proposition}Suppose that the tokenization model $({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta})$ is exact, then
\begin{equation}\forall{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}\colon\ {\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\Longleftrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\end{equation}\end{proposition}
\vskip-12.0pt
\@proof Fix an arbitrary ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}$. We consider each direction separately:
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@{Part 1} (${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}$):
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@Suppose ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}$
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$\Longrightarrow\exists{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}^{*}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}})$ by definition of ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}$
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}))={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}$ by exactness assumption
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}})))={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}})$ because ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}$ is a function
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}))={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}$ substitute ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}})\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}$
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}$ by definition of ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}$
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Thus, this direction holds.
\end{enumerate}\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@{Part 2} (${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\Longleftarrow{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}$):
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@Suppose ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}$
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}))$
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$\Longrightarrow\exists{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}^{*}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}})$; specifically, ${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})$
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}$ by definition of ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}$
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Thus, this direction holds.
\end{enumerate}\par\end{itemize}
Since each directions have been proven, the proposition holds.
\qed\end@proof
\par\begin{corollary}\@@amsalign\end{corollary}
\par\begin{proposition}Let $({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa})$ be exact. Then, $({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa})$ is a bijection between $({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}^{*}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}})$.
\end{proposition}
\vskip-12.0pt
\@proof The bijection follows directly from the following:
\par\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@Exactness means $\forall{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma}^{*}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}))={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}$.
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:the-other-half-of-the-bijection} ensures that
$\forall{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{D}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}))={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}$.
\end{itemize}
\par Thus, we have a bijection, and the proposition holds.
\qed\end@proof
\par\begin{proposition}For an exact tokenization model $({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\Sigma},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa})$, the canonicalization operator is idempotent, i.e.,
\@@amsalign
\end{proposition}
\@proof The proposition is equivalent to ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}))={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})$ for all ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}$.
\par Fix ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}^{*}}$ arbitrarily.
\par\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@Suppose ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}$
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$\Longrightarrow$ ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}))={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}$ by definition of ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}$
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$\Longrightarrow$ ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}))))={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}))$ because ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}$ are functions
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$\Longrightarrow$ ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}(\cancel{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}}(\cancel{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}))))={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}))$ by exactness
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$\Longrightarrow$ ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}))={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}))$
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$\Longrightarrow$ ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}))={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\kappa}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})))$ substitution ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})$
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$\Longrightarrow$ ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}})={\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}))$ by definition of ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\varphi}$
\end{enumerate}
\qed\end@proof

\par\par\par\par\par\lx@newpage\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@numbered@section{appendix}{toc}{Frequently Generated Noncanonical Bigrams}
\par Because BPE canonicality is determined entirely by bigram constraints (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:membership-tests}), a natural question is: \emph{Which of those constraints does a language model violate most often?} To find out, we catalogue the model\textquoteright s most frequently produced noncanonical bigrams in hopes of revealing some specific aspects of BPE canonicality that it struggles to model.
\par The {bigram frequency} is defined as
\@@amsalign
We must estimate $\mu$ because computing $\mu$ exactly is infeasible. We do so by sampling: Given a sample ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{(M)}\mathrel{\overset{\text{{{\scriptsize i.i.d.}}}}{\sim}}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Delta}}$, the following is an (unbiased) {Monte Carlo estimator}:
\@@amsalign
However, we found that using the following (unbiased) {Rao--Blackwellized Monte Carlo estimator} provided better coverage for noncanonical bigrams, as they are individually rare:
\@@amsalign
The Rao--Blackwellized estimator uses the exact conditional expectation over the token at step $t+1$ rather than the single sample token ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\delta}_{t+1}^{(m)}$, providing a significant variance reduction with little effect on the running time.
\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Experiment.}
\lx@cref{creftypepluralcap\lx@tilde refnum}{tab:gpt2-freq-nc} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{tab:llama-freq-nc} show the noncanonical bigrams (i.e., $\alpha{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\beta\notin{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\mathcal{B}}$) that we have estimated (for each model) to be the most frequent via $\widetilde{\mu}$ with $M=2000$ samples.
\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Observations and takeaways.}
Inspecting the tables, we see that a large portion of the frequently generated noncanonical bigrams are frequent substrings that have been tokenized incorrectly, for example, $\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{\textvisiblespace normal}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{3487}}}}}$}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{ized}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{1143}}}}}$}}$ corresponds to a frequent substring {\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\small{\textvisiblespace}normalized}} which---all things equal---should be tokenized as $\mbox{{\scriptsize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-4.21942pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{\mbox{\textvisiblespace normalized}}}}}}{{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{39279}}}}}$}}$. However, there does not appear to be a clear pattern for why these models struggle with these specific bigrams over others; further investigation is needed.
